(A.9)

In the second line, we used the Bianchi identity for the first term and a decomposition of contracted Weyl tensors, analogous to (
A
), for the last term. The second term in the last line vanishes because it is SD and ASD in the index pair
cd
.

Appendix B
Asymptotics at infinity

In this appendix, we prove a couple of technical results on the asymptotic geometry of ALF
S^{1}
-spaces.

B.1.
Asymptotics of Killing vector fields

We will prove that a bounded Killing field with closed orbits for a general ALF metric
g
must be asymptotic to a multiple of the Killing field
T
of the background metric
\mathring{g}
. For a similar result, see
[
6
, Proposition 2.1]
. We first formulate a lemma which is
[
17
, Lemma, Appendix A]
, adapted to our setting.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
f
be a function on
(A,\infty)\times L
with
|df|=O(r^{-1-\alpha})
,
\alpha>0
. Then there is a constant
f_{\infty}
so that
f=f_{\infty}+O(r^{-\alpha})
.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
be an ALF space. Assume
\xi^{a}
is a Killing field with uniformly bounded norm, all of whose orbits are closed.
Then

\displaystyle\xi^{a}=cT^{a}+O^{*}(r^{-1})

(B.1)

for some constant
c
and
T
has closed orbits in
L
. In particular,

\displaystyle\nabla\xi=O^{*}(r^{-2}).

(B.2)

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g=0
and
\xi
is bounded we have

\nabla^{2}\xi=\mathop{\rm Riem}\xi=O(r^{-3}),

(B.3)

see
[
50
, Proposition 8.1.3]
. Using the Kato inequality we find that

\displaystyle|d|\nabla\xi||\leq|\nabla^{2}\xi|=|\mathop{\rm Riem}\xi|=O(r^{-3}).

(B.4)

By Lemma
B.1
there is a constant
w_{\infty}
so that

\displaystyle|\nabla\xi|=w_{\infty}+O(r^{-2}).

(B.5)

and in particular
|\nabla\xi|
is bounded.

We next work in local coordinates as discussed in Remark
2.7
on

\displaystyle C\times(-t_{0},t_{0})=(A,\infty)\times U\times(-t_{0},t_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(-t_{0},t_{0})

(B.6)

for
U
an open subset of
S^{2}
. We denote the standard coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and the coordinate on
(-t_{0},t_{0})
by
x_{0}=t
. In these coordinates we have
g=\delta+dt^{2}+O(r^{-1})
. Then

\nabla\xi=d\xi+\Gamma\xi=W+O(r^{-2})

(B.7)

where
\Gamma=O(r^{-2})
represents the Christoffel symbols for
g
, and
W
is a
4\times 4
-matrix of functions representing
d\xi
. From (
B.3
) we find that

dW=\nabla^{2}\xi-\Gamma\nabla\xi=\mathop{\rm Riem}\xi-\Gamma\nabla\xi.

(B.8)

Since
\nabla\xi
is bounded we find that
|dW|=O(r^{-2})
and from Lemma
B.1
we conclude that
W=W^{\infty}+O(r^{-1})
for a constant
4\times 4
-matrix
W^{\infty}
. We thus get

\displaystyle d\xi=W^{\infty}+O(r^{-1}).

(B.9)

Since
\xi
is a Killing vector field for
g
we have

\displaystyle 0=g(\nabla_{\partial_{i}}\xi,\partial_{j})+g(\partial_{i},\nabla_{\partial_{j}}\xi)=W^{\infty}_{ij}+W^{\infty}_{ji}+O(r^{-1})

(B.10)

for
0\leq i,j\leq 3
. We conclude that
W^{\infty}
is skew-symmetric.

Let the point
p_{0}\in C
be such that the points
p_{0}+e_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
are also in
C
. Then since
C
is a cone the line segments
R(p_{0}+se_{i})
,
0\leq s\leq 1
, are also contained in
C
for
R\geq 1
. Let
c_{i}^{R}
be the curve

\displaystyle c_{i}^{R}(s)=(R(p_{0}+se_{i}),0)\subset C\times(-t_{0},t_{0}),\qquad 0\leq s\leq 1.

(B.11)

Then

\displaystyle\xi(c_{i}^{R}(1))-\xi(c_{i}^{R}(0))={}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}d\xi(Re_{i})\,ds

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle R\int_{0}^{1}d\xi(e_{i})\,ds

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle R\int_{0}^{1}\left(W^{\infty}+O(r^{-1})\right)(e_{i})\,ds

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle R(W^{\infty}_{ij}e_{j}+O(R^{-1}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle RW^{\infty}_{ij}e_{j}+O(1).

(B.12)

Since
\xi
is bounded as
R\to\infty
we find that
W^{\infty}_{ij}=0
for
i=1,2,3
. Since
W^{\infty}
is skew-symmetric we conclude that
W^{\infty}=0
.

Inserting
W=W^{\infty}+O(r^{-1})=O(r^{-1})
into (
B.7
) we get that
\nabla\xi=O(r^{-1})
, which we in turn insert into (
B.8
) to see that
|dW|=O(r^{-3})
. From Lemma
B.1
we conclude that

\displaystyle W=W^{\infty}+O(r^{-2})=O(r^{-2})

(B.13)

and

\displaystyle\nabla\xi=O(r^{-2}).

(B.14)

We next write
\xi=V
in coordinates, where
V
is a vector of functions. From

\displaystyle\nabla\xi=dV+\Gamma V

(B.15)

we see that
|dV|=O(r^{-2})
, so from Lemma
B.1
we get
V=V^{\infty}+O(r^{-1})
for a constant vector
V^{\infty}
.

In the local coordinate system we have found that

\displaystyle\xi={}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{3}V^{\infty}_{i}e_{i}+O(r^{-1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle V^{\infty}_{0}\partial_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}V^{\infty}_{i}e_{i}+O(r^{-1})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle V^{\infty}_{0}T+\sum_{i=1}^{3}V^{\infty}_{i}e_{i}+O(r^{-1}).

(B.16)

By continuity the coefficents
V^{\infty}_{0}=c
must be independent of the choice of local coordinates. Patching together with a partition of unity we find

\displaystyle\xi=cT+\eta+O(r^{-1})

(B.17)

where
\eta=Z\partial_{r}+\zeta
is a vector field on
\mathring{M}=(A,\infty)\times L
with
Z
a constant and
\zeta
a (
r
-independent) vector field on
L
, and
\mathring{g}(T,\eta)=0
.

By assumption, the orbits of
\xi
are all closed. Since
\xi
is bounded the orbits have uniformly bounded lengths. If now
\eta\neq 0
it is easy to see that
\xi
can have arbitrarily long orbits by choosing a starting point with sufficiently large
r
. We conclude that
\eta=0
and
\xi=cT+O(r^{-1})
.
Using the above estimates together with
\mathop{\rm Riem}=O^{*}(r^{-3})
and (
B.3
) we can now conclude that in fact (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) hold.

It remains to show that
T
has closed orbits on
L
. To see this, let
p\in L
be a point whose
T
-orbit is not closed, and consider a sequence of points
p_{i}=(r_{i},p)\in\mathring{\mathcal{M}}
, for
r_{i}\to\infty
. The
\xi
-orbits of
p_{i}
are closed with uniformly bounded lengths
\ell_{i}
. Passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\ell_{i}
tends to a limit
\ell_{\infty}
. Flowing
p
along
T
a time
\ell_{\infty}
yields a point
q
with
d(p,q)>0
. This contradicts (
B.1
).
∎

B.2.
Asymptotics of solutions to Poisson equations.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
be an ALF
S^{1}
instanton. In this section, we shall analyze the asymptotics at infinity for solutions of the Poisson equation of the form

\displaystyle\Delta u=O^{*}(r^{-4}),\quad\text{with $u=O^{*}(r^{-1})$}.

(B.18)

First we prove a lemma for almost invariant functions on
(\mathring{M},\mathring{g})
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
(\mathring{\mathcal{M}},\mathring{g}_{ab})
,
T
be as in Definition
2.1
. Suppose
u\in C^{2}_{loc}(\mathcal{M})
,
u=O^{*}(r^{-1})
satisfies
Tu=O^{*}(r^{-3})
and

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathring{g}}u=O^{*}(r^{-4}).

(B.19)

Then there are constants
b,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
so that

\displaystyle u=br^{-1}+O(r^{-1-\delta}).

(B.20)

Proof.

We have
\mathring{g}=dr^{2}+\sigma_{r}
where
\sigma_{r}=r^{2}\gamma+\eta^{2}
. Let
\sigma_{1}=\gamma+\eta^{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathring{g}}u=r^{-2}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r}u)+\Delta^{\sigma_{r}}u.

(B.21)

Since
T
is a Killing vector field for
\mathring{g}
we get

\displaystyle r^{2}\Delta^{\sigma_{r}}u=\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}u+(r^{2}-1)T^{2}u.

(B.22)

We thus have

\displaystyle r^{-2}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r}u)+r^{-2}\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}u+r^{-2}(r^{2}-1)T^{2}u=O(r^{-4})

(B.23)

or

\displaystyle r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}u+2r\partial_{r}u+\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}u+(r^{2}-1)T^{2}u=O(r^{-2}).

(B.24)

By assumption
Tu=O^{*}(r^{-3})
, and hence
T^{2}u=O^{*}(r^{-4})
and
(r^{2}-1)T^{2}u=O^{*}(r^{-2})
. This leads to an equation of the form

\displaystyle r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}u+2r\partial_{r}u+\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}u=f

(B.25)

for some
f
satisfying

\displaystyle f=O^{*}(r^{-2}).

(B.26)

Let
D=\sqrt{1-4\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}}
as a first order pseudodifferential operator. Then the equation factorizes as

\displaystyle r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}\partial_{r}\left(r^{1-D}\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}u\right)\right)=f,

(B.27)

or

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{1-D}\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}u\right)\right)=r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}f.

(B.28)

Since
D\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\right\|_{\operatorname{Op}(H_{s}\to H_{s})}\leq 1

(B.29)

for
r\geq 1
. Since
u=O^{*}(r^{-1})
we get

\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\partial_{r}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\partial_{r}u\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\partial_{r}u\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-2})

(B.30)

and

\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+D)u\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+D)u\right)\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+D)u\right)\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{H_{s+1}}

\displaystyle=O(r^{-1}),

(B.31)

so we find that

\displaystyle r^{1-D}\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}u\right)

\displaystyle=r^{1-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\partial_{r}u+r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1)}\left(\frac{1}{2}(1+D)u\right)

\displaystyle=rO(r^{-2})+O(r^{-1})

\displaystyle=O(r^{-1}).

(B.32)

We may thus integrate from
r
to
\infty
to conclude that

\displaystyle 0-r^{1-D}\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}u\right)=\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}f(s)\,ds

(B.33)

or

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}u\right)=-r^{D-1}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}f(s)\,ds.

(B.34)

Let

\displaystyle 1=\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}\leq\dots\leq\delta_{k}\leq 3

(B.35)

be the eigenvalues of
D
with value less than or equal to
3
and let
\phi_{0},\dots,\phi_{k}
be the corresponding orthonormal eigenfunctions. Set

\displaystyle u_{i}(r)=\int_{L}u\phi_{i}\,d\mu^{\sigma_{1}},\qquad f_{i}(r)=\int_{L}f\phi_{i}\,d\mu^{\sigma_{1}}.

(B.36)

Then

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(r)\right)=-r^{\delta_{i}-1}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}f_{i}(s)\,ds.

(B.37)

If
\delta_{i}<3
we integrate from
r
to
q
,
r<q
, and get

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(q)-r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(r)=-\int_{r}^{q}t^{\delta_{i}-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}f_{i}(s)\,ds\,dt

(B.38)

so

\displaystyle\left|q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(q)-r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(r)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r}^{q}t^{\delta_{i}-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}|f_{i}(s)|\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{r}^{q}t^{\delta_{i}-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}s^{-2}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{r}^{q}t^{\delta_{i}-1}t^{-\frac{1}{2}(3+\delta_{i})}\,dt

\displaystyle=C\int_{r}^{q}t^{-\frac{1}{2}(5-\delta_{i})}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C\left(r^{-\frac{1}{2}(3-\delta_{i})}-q^{-\frac{1}{2}(3-\delta_{i})}\right).

(B.39)

and we conclude that the function

q\mapsto q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\beta)}u_{i}(q)
is bounded and the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to\infty}q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(q)=A_{i}

(B.40)

exists. Taking the limit
q\to\infty
of the above we get

\displaystyle\left|A_{i}-r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}u_{i}(r)\right|\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(3-\delta_{i})}

(B.41)

or

\displaystyle u_{i}(r)=r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}A_{i}+O(r^{-2}).

(B.42)

If
\delta_{i}=3
we integrate from
1
to
r
to get

\displaystyle r^{2}u_{i}(r)-u_{i}(1)=-\int_{1}^{r}t^{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-2}f_{i}(s)\,ds\,dt

(B.43)

so

\displaystyle\left|r^{2}u_{i}(r)-u_{i}(1)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{1}^{r}t^{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-2}|f_{i}(s)|\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{1}^{r}t^{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-2}s^{-2}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{1}^{r}t^{2}t^{-3}\,dt

\displaystyle=C\ln r

(B.44)

and

\displaystyle u_{i}(r)=r^{-2}u_{i}(1)+O(r^{-2}\ln r)=O(r^{-2}\ln r).

(B.45)

Let
\delta
be the smallest eigenvalue of
D
which is
>3
and let
P
be the spectral projection for
D
corresponding to eigenvalues
\geq\delta
. Let
U=Pu
and
F=Pf
. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}U\right)=-r^{D-1}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}F(s)\,ds

(B.46)

which we integrate from
1
to
r
to get

\displaystyle r^{\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}U(r)-U(1)=-\int_{1}^{r}t^{D-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

(B.47)

or

\displaystyle U(r)

\displaystyle=r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}U(1)-r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{D-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=K(r)+I(r).

(B.48)

Since
D-\delta\geq 0
on the image of
P
we have

\displaystyle\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\right\|_{\operatorname{Op}(H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P\to H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P)}\leq 1

(B.49)

for
r\geq 1
. Here
\operatorname{Op}
is the operator norm. For
K(r)
we have

\displaystyle K(r)=r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}U(1)=r^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}U(1)

(B.50)

so

\displaystyle\left\|K(r)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left\|r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}U(1)\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left\|U(1)\right\|_{H_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}.

(B.51)

For
I(r)
we have

\displaystyle I(r)

\displaystyle=-r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{D-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{1}^{r}\int_{t}^{\infty}r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}t^{D-1}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+D)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{1}^{r}\int_{t}^{\infty}r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta+\delta+1)}t^{D-\delta+\delta-1}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta+\delta+1)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{1}^{r}\int_{t}^{\infty}r^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}t^{\delta-1}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}F(s)r^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}t^{D-\delta}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}F(s)\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=-r^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}F(s)\,ds\,dt.

(B.52)

Since
s\geq t
and
t\leq r
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\right\|_{\operatorname{Op}(H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P\to H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P)}\leq{}

\displaystyle 1,

(B.53)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\right\|_{\operatorname{Op}(H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P\to H_{s}\cap\operatorname{Im}P)}\leq{}

\displaystyle 1,

(B.54)

and since
F(r)=O(r^{-2})
,

\displaystyle\left\|I(r)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq r^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left\|\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}F(s)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left\|\left(\frac{r}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-\delta)}F(s)\right\|_{H_{s}}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left\|F(s)\right\|_{H_{s}}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}s^{-2}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+5)}\,ds\,dt

\displaystyle=Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\delta-1}t^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+3)}\,dt

\displaystyle=Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\int_{1}^{r}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\delta-5)}\,dt

\displaystyle=Cr^{-\frac{1}{2}(\delta+1)}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}(\delta-3)}-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-2}.

(B.55)

Together we have

\displaystyle U(r)=O(r^{-2}).

(B.56)

Finally,

\displaystyle u(r)

\displaystyle=u_{0}(r)\phi_{0}+\sum_{0<\delta_{i}<3}u_{i}(r)\phi_{i}+\sum_{\delta_{i}=3}u_{i}(r)\phi_{i}+U(r)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}A_{0}\phi_{0}+\sum_{0<\delta_{i}<3}\left(r^{-\frac{1}{2}(1+\delta_{i})}A_{i}+O(r^{-2})\right)\phi_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\delta_{i}=3}O(r^{-2}\ln r)\phi_{i}+O(r^{-2})

\displaystyle=br^{-1}+O(r^{-1-\delta})

(B.57)

for some
\delta>0
.
∎

Next we consider the general case of
S^{1}
-invariant functions on an
S^{1}
-ALF manifold.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be an
S^{1}
-ALF manifold where the circle action is generated by the bounded Killing field
\xi
. Suppose
u=O^{*}(r^{-1})
is an
S^{1}
-invariant solution to

\displaystyle\Delta^{g}u=O^{*}(r^{-4}).

(B.58)

Then there are constants
\delta>0
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
so that

\displaystyle u=br^{-1}+O(r^{-1-\delta}).

(B.59)

Proof.

Since
u=O(r^{-1})
and
\xi u=0
it follows from Proposition
B.2
that
Tu=O(r^{-3})
. From
g=\mathring{g}+O(r^{-1})
and the standard formula for the Laplacian

\displaystyle\Delta^{g}u=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\partial_{i}\left(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\partial_{j}u\right)

(B.60)

we see that
\Delta^{\mathring{g}}u=O^{*}(r^{-4})
. The result thus follows from Lemma
B.3
.
∎

Appendix C
The quotient

C.1.
Generalities

In this section we consider the orbit space
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}/S^{1}
, on which the family of divergence identities was found originally

[
53
,
54
]
. We recall its algebraic derivation from these papers; as to its relation to the lifted family (
5.22
) we make use of
[
41
]
.
However, before doing this we give a mathematically sound introduction of the orbit space.
The key is the following result

Theorem C.1

(
[
56
]
)
.

For a compact Lie group
G
acting smoothly on
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
the following holds.

(1)

There exists a unique maximal orbit type.

(2)

The union
\mathcal{M}_{0}
of maximal orbits is open and dense in
\mathcal{M}
.

(3)

The
G
- action on
\mathcal{M}
restricts to
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0}/G

is a Riemannian submersion. It is also a fibre bundle with fibre G/H where
H
is a principal isotropy group.

(4)

The quotient
\mathcal{N}_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}/G
of the principal part is open, dense and connected in
\mathcal{M}/G
.

In the present case we have
G=H=S^{1}
.
Maximal orbits in the above sense all have generic period in the sense of Lemma
3.1
.
Orbits which are not maximal are called exceptional.
While all orbits in a neighborhood of a bolt have generic period, there are exceptional orbits in the neighborhood of a non-self dual nut.

We now recall
[
22
]
that, for an
S^{1}
-isometry with Killing field
\xi^{a}
, there is a local diffeomorphism between

•

tensor fields and differential forms on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
and

•

tensor fields and forms on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
which are orthogonal to
\xi^{a}
and commute with
\xi^{a}
.

With a slight abuse of notation, we shall say that latter objects are also “on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
” (that is we drop the term “push-forward”). For objects which are defined directly on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
we use greek indices
(\alpha,\beta...=1,2,3)
.

In particular, we obtain a non-degenerate metric
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
(
\mu
,
\nu
= 1,2,3) on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
via

\gamma_{ab}=\lambda g_{ab}-\xi_{a}\xi_{b}.

(C.1)

We denote by
D_{\mu}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}
and
\mathcal{R}
the covariant derivative, the Ricci tensor and Ricci scalar with respect to
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
.

In local coordinates
x^{a}=(t,x^{\mu})
adapted to the isometry this reduction corresponds to a decomposition of
g_{ab}
as

ds^{2}=\lambda(dt+\sigma_{\mu}dx^{\mu})^{2}+\lambda^{-1}\gamma_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}

(C.2)

where
\sigma_{\mu}
is related to the pullbacks of
\omega_{a}
and

\epsilon_{abcd}\xi^{d}
to
\mathcal{N}_{0}
by

\omega_{\mu}=-\lambda^{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\tau}D^{\nu}\sigma^{\tau}.

(C.3)

In the coordinates introduced above, 1-forms
V_{a}
on
\mathcal{M}_{0}
take the form
(0,V_{\mu})
and the dual vector fields split as
V^{a}=(0,\lambda V^{\mu})
. The volume forms are related via
\sqrt{\det g}=\lambda^{-1}\sqrt{\det\gamma}
. For the divergence operators
\nabla_{a}
and
D_{\mu}
acting on vectors
V^{a}
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
we obtain

\nabla_{a}V^{a}=\lambda D_{\mu}V^{\mu}.

(C.4)

From (
C.3
) it follows that

D_{\mu}(\lambda^{-2}\omega^{\mu})=0.

(C.5)

which corresponds to the earlier observation that the current (
4.15a
) is conserved.

Excluding the half-flat case we recall from (
3
) of Lemma
5.1
that
|{{\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}|<1
on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
, and we introduce
w
,
\Theta
,
A_{\mu}
via

\displaystyle{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}={}

\displaystyle(1+{{\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}})^{-1}(1-{{\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}})

(C.6)

\displaystyle\Theta={}

Remark C.2
.

(1)

As discussed in
[
25
, Sect. 5]
, equations (
C.10
) and (
C.11
) are invariant under the (Geroch-)
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-group
[
22
]
which can be represented by the shift
\omega\rightarrow\omega+a
with fixed
\lambda
, the rescalings
{{\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}\rightarrow b{{\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}
and the Ehlers transform
{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}\rightarrow c^{\pm 1}{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}
with constants

,

and

. The Noether currents corresponding to these symmetries on

are given by (

).

(2)

A special class of solutions is characterized by
{{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{516533}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{583338}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}}}}{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{516533}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{583338}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}}}}={{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{429219}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{519722}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}}}}}{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{429219}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{519722}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}}}}}
for some constants
{{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This includes the cases in which one of
{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}
(and hence
{{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\mp$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\mp$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\mp$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mp$}}}}}}}
) vanishes identically, which have been called “Multi-NUT”, see
[

, (3.19)]
. From (
C.11
),
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
is flat for these solutions and using (
C.7
) and (
C.8
) we see that (
C.10
) reduces to an ordinary Laplace equation for the non-vanishing
{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}
. The generic solutions where
{{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{516533}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{583338}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}}}}{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{516533}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{583338}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}}}}={{d}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{429219}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{519722}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}}}}}{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{429219}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{519722}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}}}}}

C.2.
The divergence identity on the quotient

We now recall the derivation of the family of divergence identities on the quotient
\mathcal{N}_{0}
. We first introduce the pairs of quantities

Definition C.3
.

\displaystyle{{k}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}^{4}={}

(C.14)

Proof.

We first sketch the straightforward calculation in the case
\alpha=0

Remark C.6
.

(1)

The identity (
C.5
) is equivalent to (
5.22
) for
\alpha=2\beta-1
; hence remarks made in Section
5
on the latter family for various values of
\beta
carry over to the former.

(2)

For
\alpha=3
the right hand side of (
C.5
) simplifies to
\frac{1}{8}\Theta^{3}{{B}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}{{B}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}^{\mu\nu}/{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}^{3}
.

(3)

When
\xi^{a}
is hypersurface-orthogonal (which implies
\omega\equiv 0
, and
{{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.76923pt}{\resizebox{481531}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{516533}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.57141pt}{\resizebox{583338}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}}}}}={{w}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{392747}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{429219}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{519722}{262143}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}}}}}=w
) the objects
{{B}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}
coincide and we have

{{B}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}=B_{\mu\nu}=-4\Theta^{-1}w^{-1}\left(1\pm\Theta^{1/2}\right){{\mathcal{R}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}

(C.23)

where
{{\mathcal{R}}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}
are the Ricci tensors with respect to the metrics
{{\gamma}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4}(\Theta^{-1/2}\pm 1)^{2}\gamma_{\mu\nu}
.

(4)

Still for
\omega\equiv 0
, each of
{{C}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu\sigma}
reduces, via the field equations (
C.10
), (
C.11
) to the Bach–Cotton tensor whose vanishing characterizes conformal flatness. The corresponding characterizations of the Lorentzian Schwarzschild metric and the restriction of (
C.5
) for certain values of
\alpha
were employed in uniqueness proofs
[
33
,
44
]
. For example, for
\alpha=0
(
C.5
) reduces to a linear combination of
[
44
, Eq. (2.12)-(2.13)]

(5)

In the general case
{{B}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu}
,
{{k}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}
and
{{C}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\mu\nu\sigma}
have complex Lorentzian counterparts
B_{\mu\nu}
,
k
and
C_{\mu\nu\sigma}
which have analogous properties. They have been employed in local characterizations of the Kerr metric among the AF ones
[
53
]
and of larger classes of metrics if the asymptotic assumption is dropped, see
[
45
]
and references therein.

C.3.
The divergence identities on
\mathcal{M}

We can now give an alternative proof of the divergence identitity (
5.22
).

Proof.

In suitable coordinates, both sides of (
5.22
) are analytic on
{{U}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\epsilon}
and agree with the respective sides of (
C.5
) on the subset
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap{{U}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\epsilon}
which is dense, open and connected by Theorem
C.1
. Hence the validity of (
5.22
) extends to
{{U}^{\hskip-0.5pt\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.67308pt}{\resizebox{427220}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.83334pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{451967}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.40833pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}}}}{\raisebox{-0.49998pt}{\resizebox{510420}{229376}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}}}}}_{\epsilon}
as required.
∎

Remark C.7
.

The family (
C.5
) suggests an attempt for direct integration on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
, without going the detour via
\mathcal{M}
. In fact, this would directly yield relations (
7.2
) which are just what is used in the uniqueness arguments. There are problems with this strategy, however, which can be exemplified via the definition of the nut charge: Recall that on
\mathcal{M}
,
N
is defined as the flux of (
4.15a
) through a hypersurface which may contain or intersect both maximal as well as exceptional orbits on
\mathcal{M}
; accordingly
N
it is not related in an obvious way to an analogous quantity defined from (
C.5
) via a surface integral on
\mathcal{N}_{0}
. This applies in particular to the key relation (
4.25
) for the surface gravities of the nut. To see this in concrete terms, relation (
7.2
) would arise via integration of (
C.5
) on
\mathcal{N}
with a “quotient nut charge” given by
N\kappa^{1}/2\pi=1/4\kappa^{2}
, where
|\kappa^{1}|\geq|\kappa^{2}|
, that is via division of
N
by the length of the
longer exceptional
orbit. In contrast, the “quotient nut charge”
[
25
, Eq. (5.9)]
is equal to
N
divided by the length of the
maximal
orbit and thus reads
N\kappa^{1}/2\pi w^{1}=1/(4\kappa^{2}w^{1})=1/(4\kappa^{1}w^{2})
. In short, the integration procedure on
\mathcal{N}
is bound to fail without proper understanding of the integration process on the orbit space. We will not expose this in this work.
